Yeah… I think so, a bit more. I’m still keeping it together at work, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but since my father-in-law moved in I’m more wound up at home. I get shorter than I’d like, and when I try to sit down for coursework, my head just doesn’t clear as easily.

I guess… I’m not falling apart or anything, but since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve noticed I’m more wound up than usual. I can keep it together fine at work, but at home, and with my coursework, I’ve been shorter with people than I’d like.\n\nAnd I’ve kind of stopped doing the things that usually keep me level—fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. I figured I should maybe talk to someone before it turns into a bigger problem, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities because of your father-in-law moving in?\n\nPatient: Not places or people so much, I don’t think. If I should be paying closer attention to that, I can.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad or down most of the time, no. I mean, I have moments where I’m frustrated or kind of tired of the situation, but not like I’m depressed all day or anything. At least, that’s not how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you used to since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, a bit more. I’m still keeping it together at work, so I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but since my father-in-law moved in I’m more wound up at home. I get shorter than I’d like, and when I try to sit down for coursework, my head just doesn’t clear as easily.\n\nAnd I guess I’ve let the things that usually help with that—fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops—slide more than I normally would. So, yes, I’m worrying or carrying it around more than I used to, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll replay a conversation at home sometimes or think about what I should’ve said differently, but I wouldn’t call it something I can’t get out of my head. It’s more just… normal stress, I guess. If that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts or urges that keep repeating and feel uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stuck on household stuff sometimes, but not like urges or thoughts that feel out of my control. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your father-in-law or the situation at home repeatedly, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not all the time, and I can still do my job, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart. But when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, my mind keeps drifting back to the situation with my father-in-law being there.\n\nIt’s more like I’m wound up and short with people, and then I think about that afterward. And I know I haven’t been doing the things that usually clear my head—fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. So… yes, it’s on my mind more than I’d like, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling wound up and struggling to manage your stress at home since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I’d say. Basically since my father-in-law moved into our house. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s when I started feeling more wound up at home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or interactions with your father-in-law that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding exactly. I mean, I don’t go out of my way to pick fights or anything. Sometimes I’ll just keep things short or busy myself with something else if I think it’s going to turn into a tense conversation. But I haven’t really noticed a specific situation I’m deliberately avoiding, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily routine or overall functioning since you started feeling wound up at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, overall I’m still functioning. I’m still showing up for my EMT shifts and doing what I need to do there, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut… yeah, there has been some impact. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which I’m not proud of. And I haven’t been showing up as consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're repeating certain behaviors or rituals over and over to try and relieve stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I might double-check things like anyone does, especially with work being what it is, but I wouldn’t say I have rituals or anything I feel like I have to repeat to calm down. I haven’t really noticed that, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with family or friends outside of your household have been affected by your stress at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I might be a little less talkative sometimes, or I’ll keep a phone call shorter if I’m already feeling tense, but I haven’t had any falling-outs or anything like that.\n\nAs far as I can tell, my relationships outside the house are still okay. I hope that sounds fair—I’m trying not to make it sound better than it is, but I really haven’t noticed a big effect there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. When I’m at work, I can usually keep it together pretty well, and if I’m not at home or not focused on the situation with my father-in-law, I’m not as wound up.

It’s more when I get back home—or when I’m trying to do coursework at home—that I notice I’m shorter than usual and more tense. I guess I’ve also let some of the things that usually help me clear my head slide, like fishing or woodworking, even the first-aid workshops. I don’t know if that counts as improvement when I’m away, but it does seem tied pretty closely to being at home.

I get shorter than I’d like, and when I try to sit down for coursework, my head just doesn’t clear as easily.\n\nAnd I guess I’ve let the things that usually help with that—fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops—slide more than I normally would. So, yes, I’m worrying or carrying it around more than I used to, if that’s the right way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll replay a conversation at home sometimes or think about what I should’ve said differently, but I wouldn’t call it something I can’t get out of my head. It’s more just… normal stress, I guess. If that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having thoughts or urges that keep repeating and feel uncontrollable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stuck on household stuff sometimes, but not like urges or thoughts that feel out of my control. At least I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your father-in-law or the situation at home repeatedly, even when you're trying to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some, I guess. Not all the time, and I can still do my job, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart. But when I’m home, or when I’m trying to sit down and do coursework, my mind keeps drifting back to the situation with my father-in-law being there.\n\nIt’s more like I’m wound up and short with people, and then I think about that afterward. And I know I haven’t been doing the things that usually clear my head—fishing, woodworking, the first-aid workshops. So… yes, it’s on my mind more than I’d like, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling wound up and struggling to manage your stress at home since your father-in-law moved in?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about four months, I’d say. Basically since my father-in-law moved into our house. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s when I started feeling more wound up at home.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific situations or interactions with your father-in-law that you try to avoid?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it avoiding exactly. I mean, I don’t go out of my way to pick fights or anything. Sometimes I’ll just keep things short or busy myself with something else if I think it’s going to turn into a tense conversation. But I haven’t really noticed a specific situation I’m deliberately avoiding, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily routine or overall functioning since you started feeling wound up at home?\n\nPatient: I mean, overall I’m still functioning. I’m still showing up for my EMT shifts and doing what I need to do there, so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut… yeah, there has been some impact. I’ve fallen behind on some of my paramedic coursework, which I’m not proud of. And I haven’t been showing up as consistently for the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track, but it’s been harder than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're repeating certain behaviors or rituals over and over to try and relieve stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I might double-check things like anyone does, especially with work being what it is, but I wouldn’t say I have rituals or anything I feel like I have to repeat to calm down. I haven’t really noticed that, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your relationships with family or friends outside of your household have been affected by your stress at home?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, I might be a little less talkative sometimes, or I’ll keep a phone call shorter if I’m already feeling tense, but I haven’t had any falling-outs or anything like that.\n\nAs far as I can tell, my relationships outside the house are still okay. I hope that sounds fair—I’m trying not to make it sound better than it is, but I really haven’t noticed a big effect there.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your stress at home has significantly impacted your ability to perform your job as an EMT or complete your paramedic coursework?\n\nPatient: I’d say my EMT work is still okay—I’m showing up and doing the job, and I don’t think I’ve let that slip in any serious way.\n\nThe coursework is where it’s been more noticeable. I’ve fallen behind on some of the paramedic stuff, which… I don’t feel great admitting. And I haven’t been as consistent with the community first-aid workshops I usually help with. So, yes, it’s affected things some, just not to the point where I’m not functioning at work, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're unable to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like because of feeling wound up at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say, unfortunately. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely shut down or anything, because I’m still getting through work, but at home I’ve been more wound up and short than I’m used to.\n\nAnd the stuff that normally helps me clear my head—fishing, woodworking, helping with the first-aid workshops—I’ve been skipping more of that. Some of it is just feeling tense after things at home, since my father-in-law moved in with us, and some of it is falling behind on coursework and feeling like I shouldn’t take the time. So… yes, I guess I haven’t been participating in those things the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social interactions because of your father-in-law's presence?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people completely, but since my father-in-law moved in, I’m more tense at home and I’m less likely to go do the things that usually get me around people.\n\nLike I’ve been skipping the first-aid workshops sometimes, and I haven’t been fishing or doing woodworking as much either. I’m still holding it together at work, but outside of that I’ve been more wound up and short than I’d like to be. I guess that counts as avoiding some interaction, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind and feel like you can't control them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I think about the situation at home more than I’d like sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s like thoughts repeating that I can’t control.\n\nUnless you mean just worrying about it? Because I do that some, but it doesn’t feel like… intrusive or anything like that. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve your stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ll try to keep busy or step away for a minute if I’m stressed, but I don’t have any rituals or specific things I feel like I have to do to make the anxiety go down.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of feeling wound up and struggling to manage stress at home lasted for more than six months?\n\nPatient: No, not more than six months. It’s been, I’d say, about four months now—basically since my father-in-law moved into our house.\n\nSo it hasn’t been that long, even if it’s felt like a lot at times. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant improvement in your stress levels when you're away from home or not thinking about your father-in-law?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I’m at work, I can usually keep it together pretty well, and if I’m not at home or not focused on the situation with my father-in-law, I’m not as wound up.\n\nIt’s more when I get back home—or when I’m trying to do coursework at home—that I notice I’m shorter than usual and more tense. I guess I’ve also let some of the things that usually help me clear my head slide, like fishing or woodworking, even the first-aid workshops. I don’t know if that counts as improvement when I’m away, but it does seem tied pretty closely to being at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
